## Finding 235 — complete export snapshot foundation

Five preceding-route regressions reproduce REST-cap truncation, unrelated CSV reads, querying before invalid-table rejection, raw database errors inside successful downloads and retired-table fallback after an archive failure. These tests use only synthetic owners and isolated PostgreSQL/SDK transport; the transport explicitly simulates the REST thousand-row cap. They are failing baseline evidence, not passing route verification.

Canonical CLI-created migration 122 prepares one STABLE captured-owner export RPC. It retains invoker RLS and authenticated-only execution, uses positive table and column lists, deterministic primary-key order and the caller's statement snapshot. It preflights bounded projected rows before allocating their aggregate, refuses more than 10,000 rows/table or a 10 MiB envelope, and never returns partial data. New columns cannot silently join downloads. This is not a streaming guarantee; individual projected values still require database memory. It introduces no new policy/index, service-role access or owner-record write.

Twenty final database checks pass in 2.07 seconds (1.93 seconds in tests); targeted ESLint passes. They cover all forty-six table/column contracts, complete 1,201-row ownership/order, mismatched/missing owners, invalid/duplicate/retired tables, anon/service denial, function volatility/privileges, future-column exclusion and row/byte overflow. The generated read port is regenerated from all 122 canonical files. There are thirty-six prepared/unapplied files (87–122), while production remains at the last verified 86. Route/UI adoption follows separately; checkpoint 86 remains the latest consolidated baseline.


## Finding 236 — verified complete download route and CSV safety

The export endpoint now validates scope, format, duplicate/unknown options and CSV table before authentication or data reads. It requires a successful getUser result, checks an optional captured browser owner and uses one strict snapshot receipt. Combined data reads each shared table once while preserving the public account/financial/professional/knowledge grouping. CSV requests read only their selected table. An absent legacy archive is explicitly null; a failed archive read fails the download and never touches removed personal tables.

The request has a thirty-second total lifetime, an eight-second auth phase and a fifteen-second database phase, linked to caller cancellation. One server client receives a per-request phase fetcher and refuses late cookie writes after disposal. Promise races also bound transports that ignore cancellation; RPC retries are disabled. Exact envelope/table/column/owner/primary-key checks reject malformed or foreign receipts, including empty replies with missing column contracts. All success and failure responses are private/no-store with Vary Cookie and nosniff; only verified downloads get attachment headers. Database and final serialized byte overflow return a smaller-scope response without leaking diagnostics. The SDK still parses before receipt validation; this is not streaming network protection.

CSV uses stable reviewed columns, CRLF records and escaped quotes. Formula-like text receives a tab inside its quoted cell, including leading whitespace/control/BOM and fullwidth formula prefixes; finite numeric negatives remain numbers. JSON keeps raw owner data. This reduces common spreadsheet formula interpretation and deliberately changes risky CSV text; it is not a universal spreadsheet guarantee or a real Excel test. Primary guidance: [OWASP CSV Injection](https://community.owasp.org/attacks/CSV_Injection).

All 64 focused tests pass across four files in 2.67 seconds; TypeScript and targeted ESLint pass. The original five failing regressions now pass. Additional checks cover combined one-read/deduplication, archive shape/absence, auth errors with cached user, changed owner, malformed receipt/options, no transient retry, uncooperative auth/query deadlines, cancellation/late results, column projections, CSV bytes and final combined-file overflow caused by a shared table. All data and credentials are synthetic. Settings presentation/guest/download lifecycle remains next; production rollout, later app repairs and final consolidated/main/deployment checks remain unfinished.


## Finding 237 — compact owned Settings downloads

Settings replaces ten separate export anchors with one labelled dataset selector and two equal JSON/Transactions CSV actions. Its existing peer-card size stays consistent; the controls wrap according to their available card width rather than assuming a wide viewport means a wide card. EN/CS guidance names the combined dataset accurately and keeps the legacy archive separate. Pending work locks the selector and both actions synchronously. Failure retains the selection, uses fixed retry/size/account guidance and never renders a raw diagnostic. A completed action says Download started, not saved. Keyboard selection and return focus use the existing shared Select.

The browser captures the owner's lifetime, sends that id for a server-side comparison and aborts when leaving or replacing that owner. It rejects login pages, incorrect filenames/types and empty downloads, reads at most 10 MiB of file body with a forty-second deadline, cancels refused/late streams and holds only an ephemeral Blob URL. URLs are revoked after sixty seconds or on component disposal. Public guest actions announce the existing sign-in boundary once without a private request or duplicate local error. The fake-backend-only exports harness enables none of the other provider/preference integrations.

All 90 focused database, route, CSV, stream/client and mounted cases pass across six files in 5.82 seconds. TypeScript and targeted ESLint pass. Eight final native EN/CS desktop/mobile cases pass in 26.3 seconds, checking exact downloaded JSON/CSV bytes and filenames, captured owner/routes, pending and error recovery, keyboard selection/focus, equal actions and no overflow across 360/430/640/767/768/1024/1440/1728px, both themes, axe and no page errors. Guest cases prove zero export requests. Native requests use explicit synthetic API replies; the separate actual SDK/PGlite route checks establish the database boundary. This is not a real signed-in export or a production verification. An initial native harness run stops after detached dev-DOM, keyboard targeting and guest-navigation test failures; it is not reported as passing. Authentic gallery crops follow from committed application source.

An intercepted successful server receipt removes only its synthetic shared auth cookie. The other foregrounded workspace still shows private research and no recovery screen after cookie removal. The [boolean baseline](http-signout-before.json) records application 4349ce5 with no credential/cookie values. A mounted failing regression also leaves private content visible after confirmed HTTP logout (one failure/eight passes). SDK sign-out evidence from finding 238 did not establish the product's server-logout path.

After the server confirms ok:true, the shared action sends a fixed retirement-only marker through BroadcastChannel, an ephemeral set/remove storage fallback and a local event. The server-owned shell subscribes once within its existing lifetime. A received marker only retires that scope; it contains no owner, token or session and cannot authenticate or reopen access. Channels/listeners close on retirement/disposal, and restricted storage/channel creation cannot prevent the sender's local retirement. If both cross-tab transports are unavailable, only that local signal is guaranteed. The existing SDK listener remains active. Failed/guest logout emits no marker. This adds no verification, logout or provider call, preserves the single in-flight request and keeps the existing guarded full-document navigation.

All 39 focused signal/session/action/mounted cases pass across four files in 0.53 seconds; 22 navigation, sign-out recovery and owner-cache cases pass across three files in 5.22 seconds. TypeScript, targeted ESLint and whitespace checks pass. All sixteen final native EN/CS desktop/mobile session cases pass in 1.2 minutes, including normal HTTP logout and a browser without BroadcastChannel. Failed receipt preserves the dirty editor/cookie; confirmed receipt removes the cookie, private UI/cache and guarded dialog, with unchanged auth-call counts and zero record writes. Same-owner refresh retention, blocked later requests, no client-event reopening, six-width reflow, axe and full-reload recovery remain covered. These are synthetic intercepted transports, not real account/provider or production verification.

The ledger now contains 677 entries. Checkpoint 87 remains a failed consolidated run, with targeted fixture recovery recorded in finding 243; checkpoint 86 remains the latest full pass. Server endpoint authentication, remaining action/provider/invoice leads, 36 prepared migrations, final combined release verification, PR/main and deployed work remain open.

## Finding 249 — captured manual Calendar creation and bounded owned receipts

The manual Calendar form now captures its original React owner and panel lifetime before sending. It uses the captured-account endpoint and requires that same owner in a strict success receipt; replacement/unmount cancels pending work and prevents late form/reset/cache effects. Guest submission announces the shared sign-in boundary once and sends no request. Pending fields/exits remain locked, failed/uncertain drafts remain, explicit retry stays manual and expired-grant feedback stays distinct. Existing browser fixtures echo the submitted synthetic owner rather than weakening the receipt check.

A shared creation/cleanup transport makes one explicit attempt, rejects redirects, bounds the serialized request to 64KiB and reads at most a 16,384-character response prefix before strict parsing. It accepts only a verified owner/id or explicit owned cleanup receipt, omits unsafe links and exposes fixed failures. It cancels unread late response bodies as well as active readers, including when a transport ignores cancellation. It has a twenty-five-second total wait bound, captured-owner comparison and no automatic retry or credential storage. This is bounded prefix/accepted-result protection, not a guarantee about a producer's already allocated network chunk. Optional plan Calendar adoption follows.

All 52 focused client/route/mounted cases pass across three files in 2.00 seconds. TypeScript, targeted ESLint and whitespace checks pass. ## Finding 265 — retire unannounced REST cookie-owner replacement

An actual installed SSR SDK/RLS regression reproduces a second account's private note entering the first account's still-mounted cache after an HTTP cookie replacement without an SDK event (one failure, 4.18 seconds). The SDK reads its current cookie for the next request without emitting a session-change event for an unexpired session. Event retirement alone therefore leaves a reachable legacy-read gap. Only invented accounts, credentials and records are used.

The existing session boundary now inspects effective REST bearer-subject claims immediately before transport. A missing, malformed, oversized or different subject retires the original workspace, cancels protected work and clears its private query cache before sending that request. A later matching event cannot reopen it. This is a negative-only check: decoded claims grant no access, never select a replacement owner and never replace server signature verification or own-only RLS. Request/init header replacement follows fetch semantics; no user metadata is trusted. The check targets the configured backend's exact REST path, including RPCs and configured path prefixes. Auth refresh, other origins, relative APIs and narrow signed storage uploads keep their existing contracts. Relative APIs still require their own captured-owner adoption.

## Finding 284 — protect private Saved drafts and deletion with confirmed versions

An actual migrated SDK baseline reproduces loss of a newer private note: the older letter form sends its old note with an unconditional update and replaces it (one intended failure, 2.66 seconds). A second window's work must survive an older form.

`career-saved-records.ts` now captures the original owner, complete source and submitted fields before waiting. Updates/deletions require the source's database version and return one complete, strictly owned row. An update must preserve every field outside its patch and advance the six-digit database version; a deletion must return the exact old row. Zero rows mean conflict rather than success. Cache application cannot restore a missing row or replace newer/foreign content. Existing migration 128 supplies monotonic versions; no new migration is introduced.

Creation uses a stable caller id and an insert. On a uniqueness conflict it may read only that same owned id, requiring every submitted/default field to match. A different row at the same URL stays untouched and returns a conflict; no upsert resets its notes, letter, remote evidence or readiness. Calls have one fifteen-second deadline, parent cancellation, no automatic retry and a disposed local lifetime. Errors expose fixed text, not database details. Positive projections exclude future private columns. New submitted fields have explicit limits, a 256 KiB encoded payload bound, normalized credential-free HTTP(S) URLs and validated Google letter links. Unchanged legacy text has no new-write limit applied to it. The existing provider contract permits a 300-character title. URL storage validation makes no claim of SSRF protection for enrichment.

The [current Supabase update](https://supabase.com/docs/reference/javascript/update) and [delete](https://supabase.com/docs/reference/javascript/delete) documentation confirm filtered operations and explicit returning selection; the changelog was reviewed, including the already-tracked November TLS change. All 87 focused cases pass in five files (10.38 seconds), covering 35 new client/database cases plus private versions and discovery saves. Actual full-chain SDK/RLS checks preserve a newer note, duplicate-URL private history and oversized legacy description; recover an identical stable create; refuse a changed retry, foreign row and stale/absent deletion; confirm a one-microsecond update; and exclude a newly added private column. Injected transports cover malformed/expanded receipts, cancellation, the total deadline, immutable pending inputs, invalid/oversized intent, no retry and late cache protection. TypeScript, targeted ESLint and whitespace checks pass after widening a test fixture’s initially null note type. No real owner record or external provider is used.

This is a shared-write foundation, not completion of its browser callers. Manual URL/Saved/letter controls, the default visibility of a manual unknown-location save, captured application conversion and other recorded leads remain open. ## Finding 285 — bound URL import and bind metadata to the original owner

The URL-import route reproduces provider work after an authentication error with a cached SDK user (expected 503, actual 200). It now checks authentication errors, validates the optional captured-owner header, and stops changed/invalid owners before quota/provider work. Input is a strict URL-only object: no draft, profile or private notes are sent to Jina, and no model API is introduced. The unchanged admission remains twenty imports per minute. Canonical normalized URL length is checked after UTF-8 percent encoding, as well as before parsing.

The route has a twenty-five-second total lifetime: three seconds/16 KiB for request reading, five seconds for client/auth admission, three seconds for quota and one fifteen-second provider phase covering both headers and body. Upstream JSON is capped at 1 MiB; known metadata types and the 300-character title/500-character company contracts are validated before returning an explicit owner/input-URL receipt. Description output retains its existing 30,000-character limit. Provider metadata not selected for the response stays private; invalid URLs/fields and provider errors produce fixed unavailable responses. Failed, oversized and late response bodies are disposed. Responses are private/no-store.

The companion browser helper captures owner and normalized input before waiting, requires an exact positive receipt and bounds streamed bytes at 256 KiB under a thirty-second total lifetime. It neither retries nor exposes upstream diagnostics. Cancellation includes fetch/body work and ignores late responses. A client regression exposed the narrow cancellation gap after headers had settled but before body reading began; both client and server now dispose that received body in `finally`, while a late-fetch observer covers responses arriving after retirement.

Current [Jina API documentation](https://docs.jina.ai/) confirms JSON content in `data.content`; the [official Reader implementation guide](https://github.com/jina-ai/reader/blob/main/README.md) documents `X-Respond-With: markdown`. The request uses that documented non-model format, without ReaderLM/OCR or a new provider. All provider verification is offline, using synthetic responses. No real import, account change, key read or spend occurs.

All 91 focused cases pass in five files (14.79 seconds), including the new import route/client and existing Saved contracts. TypeScript, targeted ESLint and whitespace checks pass. Browser form adoption and private Saved/letter/application controls remain separate. A new native baseline also confirms that the current manual form saves an unknown-location row but hides it under the default location filter (one expected failure, 8.7 seconds). That reachability repair must preserve the stored preference and the existing Prague archive policy. The source inventory has 692 entries. The 130 canonical local migrations/forty-four prepared files are unchanged; checkpoint 97 remains on its frozen earlier snapshot. ## Finding 290 — avoid application/source-cleanup deadlocks and align title limits

An isolated native PostgreSQL 17.11 baseline reproduces a real lock cycle: cleanup owns the public listing row and waits to null its private Saved FK, while conversion holds that Saved row and waits to reference the public row. Conversion exits with a detected deadlock; cleanup completes, leaving the private letter/note in Saved with no application/event. A separate full-chain actual SDK baseline rejects a 300-character title with `Position required`, although Career sources and new Saved records accept 300 characters (one expected failure, 3.93 seconds).

CLI-created migration 131 (`20261026163911_career_application_lock_order.sql`) keeps the legacy function, owner/RLS/request/day/event semantics and existing grants. New conversions take the existing shared source guard after idempotency lookup and before any private Saved row lock. Cleanup already takes its exclusive guard first. This follows [PostgreSQL's consistent lock-order guidance](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/17/explicit-locking.html#LOCKING-DEADLOCKS) and uses [transaction-scoped shared advisory locks](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/17/functions-admin.html#FUNCTIONS-ADVISORY-LOCKS). Already-recorded retries avoid the extra guard. Each lock wait is bounded at five seconds; this is not a total client/operation deadline. The title bound becomes 1–300 trimmed characters. No private row or table field is rewritten.

The repaired native run proves the private row is still unlocked while conversion waits for the source guard. Cleanup and conversion then both exit successfully: public/Saved rows are removed, one application and one event retain the private snapshot, and no deadlock occurs. `career-application-lock-concurrency.json` keeps baseline/fixed outcomes and exact source hashes. Its fixture uses exact stock Career tables, directory migration and migrations 125/127/128/131 plus a deterministic owner-day stand-in; it is not a full canonical native replay. The isolated cluster is stopped afterward.

All 42 focused cases pass across three full-chain database files (23.11 seconds, one worker): thirteen new SDK/RLS/title/conversion/pruned-source/ACL/upgrade/rollback cases, the sixteen private remote cases and thirteen monotonic-version cases. The first related run exposed the old exact configuration expectation; it is updated to include the intentional five-second lock bound while retaining invoker/empty-path/anon refusal. TypeScript, targeted ESLint and whitespace checks pass. The eight-table read port regenerates from all 131 files, with only its schema fingerprint changed. No provider or production record is touched.

A fresh 26 October managed count-only check confirms 86 applied migrations through `20261023130300`: all forty-five audit files (87–131) remain prepared/unapplied. The source ledger now has 694 entries. Checkpoint 98 remains frozen at `7a99650` and excludes this new SQL. Captured application/letter/progress/template lifecycles, other recorded app leads, canonical rollout, clean final verification, PR/main and deployed checks remain unfinished.

The receipt validates owner/id/stored time/status/outcome, captures input before waiting and disables SDK retries.

## Finding 311 — conservative cron freshness and explicit unknown time

Two baseline assertions reproduce weekday-average and monthly-date errors (146 ms, with twenty unrelated cases filtered out): Monday–Friday uses 1.4 days instead of its three-day weekend gap, and day 31 uses thirty days even when a month has no matching date. Supported weekday expressions now use their largest gap across the week boundary, including duplicate Sunday aliases. Supported monthly dates use their largest real Gregorian gap: 31 days for dates 1–28, then 59/60/61 for 29/30/31. Minute/hour steps retain their within-field-reset bounds. Unknown/restricted shapes keep the owner's coarse run-frequency estimate; invalid/nonfinite frequencies stay finite/positive and cannot imply sub-minute cron polling. Schedule input is bounded before parsing. No cost, scheduler, timezone, execution or network behavior changes.

A missing/invalid clock, malformed stored success or success more than five minutes ahead yields a neutral localized Not assessed state. Disabled rows remain unmonitored; small skew clamps negative age to zero. Unknowns do not count as stale project crons. The server/hydration placeholder no longer treats epoch time as proof of a healthy recent run. Keyboard tooltips describe these values as estimates from the longest usual gap or saved frequency, keeping actual external monitoring confirmation separate. The [Cronie manual](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man5/crontab.5.html) confirms weekday aliases, inclusive ranges, within-field steps, day matching and timezone/DST limits; the helper remains a conservative estimator rather than a scheduler.

All 109 focused checks pass across four files in 5.17 seconds. They cover every nonempty weekday set, actual calendar-date enumeration over 400 years, step resets, monthly skipped dates, invalid/future clocks, unchanged disabled/small-skew behavior and the Kuma client/route boundaries. All ten final native cases pass across two files in 3.3 minutes: EN/CS desktop/mobile, keyboard details, all six widths, axe and existing project operations/communication/untouched-note behavior. Service clients are created only inside the admitted execution phase with that phase's signal. Foreground render/record work shares request cancellation and the existing 280-second limit. Signed `after()` work creates its own bounded scope after acknowledgement, so the acknowledged caller closing does not retire authorized background execution. ### Finding 353 — complete bounded linked-Page lookup

Four initial cases fail in 382ms: a valid empty first page hides a later linked Page, multiple matching Pages choose the first, a failed list can be masked by a fallback success, and verification misses its configured handle beyond the first response. Both Page-token resolution and verification now traverse declared continuation under their existing original consumer/request bounds, with ten pages of 25 rows maximum. A continued result past the cap stays unavailable. Empty continued pages do not imply completion; malformed/duplicate/overlapping rows and repeated or contradictory cursors refuse. Matching must be unique across the observed list.

Every continuation rebuilds the original known endpoint and fields with only the validated cursor. Foreign paths/origins, credentials/fragments, duplicate cursor parameters and mismatched cursor sources refuse before another request. Credential-bearing provider next URLs are never followed or copied. Only the initial HTTP 400 may use the existing Page-token /me fallback; later failures, malformed lists, rate limits and server failures remain failures. The provider list is not an immutable snapshot against changes between requests.

The [official Meta SDK cursor](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/facebook/facebook-nodejs-business-sdk/main/src/cursor.js) uses the presence of paging.next to identify continuation. Rebuilding the request and validating its cursor are local hardening choices. Direct official documentation pages returned HTTP 429 during this check; no third-party endpoint guidance is used to change the existing login endpoints or permissions.

### Finding 354 — captured verification transaction foundation (28 October)

Three actual SDK/full-chain races first fail in 1.88 seconds: verification overwrites an account edited, recreated or created while the provider waits. A fourth credential-rotation regression joins the baseline; all four fail in 2.28 seconds. They remain outside the passing foundation set until caller adoption.

Migration 146 prepares a service-only security-invoker transaction over the existing private credential reader/writer. It captures owner, exact account absence or id/full version, and a private credential fingerprint. The fingerprint binds token bytes and metadata even if public timestamps/scopes happen to match. Its tuple advisory → account UPDATE → credential lock order matches the existing writer. Stale/removed/recreated/newly present/rotated state returns changed before writing. Fingerprints and credentials never occur in receipts. No table privileges, policies, indexes or existing rows change when this function is prepared/applied.

After the legacy atomic writer, the wrapper rereads the actual account and credential. It verifies the exact remote identity, finite advancing account version, unchanged unrelated/future fields, correct newer-token retention and the actual stored token even when public metadata matches. Suppressed/rewritten writes and private failures roll back with fixed diagnostics. An unchanged identity keeps both switches; a changed remote identity disables publishing and replies. The exact stored/retained token remains private. [PostgreSQL's built-in SHA-256 and encoding functions](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/17/functions-binarystring.html) provide the private comparison without a new extension.

All 2,302 tracked related cases pass across 126 files in 192.47 seconds with one worker. The first broad run has 2,300 passes/two timeouts (golden PNGs and an optional audio wait). Both unchanged files pass all 20 cases in 4.57 seconds; the final broad run above passes without changing deadlines. No cause is asserted for those timeouts.

A separate source-level baseline fails in 1.45 seconds (six unrelated cases skipped): a valid 200 response reporting a transient error for a previously readable calendar also evicts its confirmed content. Plan rows, usage counts and Work's previous checked-at label survive; explicit successful recovery replaces the snapshot and clears the warning. A temporary source error/too-large/invalid-JSON/wrong-schema outcome retains the whole prior snapshot, rather than mixing old contents with a new global checkedAt. A recorded 429 uses rate guidance. Confirmed empty data, missing/disconnected/unreadable/wrong-project outcomes are adopted; Dashboard sign-out replaces cached private data. This conservative snapshot policy can temporarily withhold another calendar's newer update, which the notice identifies as previous plans. No automatic error retry, new polling, extra request or persisted cache is added.

All 58 related cases pass across four files in 4.95 seconds. ### Finding 388 — outer-window scrolling investigation (in progress)

Checkpoint 101's Lab comparison exposes a real scroll boundary: scrollIntoView moves the desktop mac-window by 56px, carrying both its toolbar and sidebar header outside the frame. A separate EN/CS baseline reproduces that exact offset. The draft overflow-clip repair keeps the outer offset at zero through six-width scrolling/focus checks (four desktop/touch cases, 24.3 seconds), but the existing approval/field checks then expose unloaded offscreen saved-deck thumbnails at 768/1024px. A controlled 768px browser probe shows three of four thumbnails incomplete; making only the selected deck eager loads all four and enables the original approval gate. Source-pool images are not changed.

This repair is not accepted yet. The first 24-case clip-only matrix has 16 passes/eight approval-readiness failures in 2.4 minutes. After the selected-deck change, the 26-case attempt is interrupted after 12 passes, one failed English 768px readiness assertion and one interrupted navigation; twelve cases do not run. Two related unit attempts also fail different unchanged Costs mount/refresh assertions (68 passes/one failure, then 34 passes/one failure). Their causes remain under investigation; no timing, image comparison or readiness assertion is relaxed. The native failure remains visible even though an ad-hoc loading probe succeeds. Checkpoint 101 stays frozen and excludes this draft; its original image failures are retained. Final screenshots and the full affected checks remain pending.

An unchanged traced optimized reproduction of checkpoint 101's English Work health failure reaches the actual midnight/heartbeat and project-workspace assertions, then waits for a missing Career link. ### Finding 390 — await the Costs fixture's actual account read

The two finding-388 unit attempts fail different unchanged Costs assertions. The parent cost read must commit before its account query mounts. A single 400ms act-wrapped sleep can finish that parent commit while the nested account read is still pending; the test then either inspects a missing token time or clicks a legitimately disabled Refresh. The fixture now awaits the actual existing account-query promise and its notification before those checks. Expected read failures are still asserted by their original recovery cases. No timeout increases, arbitrary extra sleep, product source, query policy or count assertion changes.

All 69 related tests pass across three files in 27.07 seconds, including all 35 workspace cases. Full TypeScript with the documented local 4GB allowance, targeted ESLint without warnings and whitespace pass. The original 68-pass/one-failure and 34-pass/one-failure attempts remain recorded in finding 388. Its eight unchanged middle-width native cases separately pass in 59.4 seconds after the selected-deck loading change; the complete affected native matrix and optimized final release remain separate.
